the thoughts just keep looping and branching off, and no matter how much I tell myself to let it go or distract myself, I can’t seem to shut it off for more than a few minutes. \n- S059 What brings you in?\n\nPatient: My mind is constantly racing about the practice and the future, and I just can’t shut the worrying off anymore. I'm trying to plan my retirement, but instead of feeling excited, I'm just constantly consumed by what-ifs about the business.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this constant, hard-to-control worry—has it been most days for at least 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has been going on pretty much every day for about a year and a quarter now. It really started when I began planning my retirement and trying to arrange the practice buy-in with the younger dentists, and it hasn't let up since.\n\nDoctor: When you’re worrying, do you also get symptoms like restlessness, feeling on edge, muscle tension, trouble concentrating, irritability, or sleep problems?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. I'm barely sleeping because I lie awake running numbers and wake up at 3:00 AM unable to drift back off, leaving me feeling constantly keyed up and physically drained. My anxiety is more of a constant, persistent background worry and a mind that just won't stop racing. It is definitely impacting my life, as I've started making small charting mistakes at work, turning down social invitations because I'm so drained, and my wife says I'm barely present even when we're sitting in the same room.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year, have you also had periods of feeling down, losing interest or pleasure in things, or feeling hopeless?"

It is a steady, ongoing pressure rather than sudden bursts of terror.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the worry is excessive and hard to control even when there is no immediate problem, and is it causing problems in your daily life or work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it feels completely excessive and uncontrollable, constantly looping through \"what-if\" scenarios even when there's no immediate crisis. It is definitely impacting my life, as I've started making small charting mistakes at work, turning down social invitations because I'm so drained, and my wife says I'm barely present even when we're sitting in the same room.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year, have you also had periods of feeling down, losing interest or pleasure in things, or feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I feel hopeless or deeply depressed, but I have definitely lost enjoyment in my work. I've also stopped doing things I used to love, like hiking, painting, and socializing, because I am constantly too worried, keyed up, and exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks where you felt depressed most of the day or lost interest/pleasure in nearly everything, not just avoided activities because of worry?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really felt depressed or lost interest in that way. My lack of engagement in things like hiking or painting is purely because I am constantly exhausted and too consumed by worry about the practice, rather than feeling down."

What brings you in?\n\nPatient: My mind is constantly racing about the practice and the future, and I just can’t shut the worrying off anymore. I'm trying to plan my retirement, but instead of feeling excited, I'm just constantly consumed by what-ifs about the business.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this constant, hard-to-control worry—has it been most days for at least 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has been going on pretty much every day for about a year and a quarter now. I'm barely sleeping because I lie awake running numbers and wake up at 3:00 AM unable to drift back off, leaving me feeling constantly keyed up and physically drained. My concentration has also taken a hit; I'll actually lose my train of thought or go completely blank right in the middle of a dental procedure.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have sudden episodes of intense fear or panic that peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, shortness of breath, chest pain, or feeling like something terrible is about to happen?\n\nPatient: No, I don't experience those sudden, intense spikes of panic or physical symptoms like chest pain and shortness of breath. My anxiety is more of a constant, persistent background worry and a mind that just won't stop racing. It is a steady, ongoing pressure rather than sudden bursts of terror.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the worry is excessive and hard to control even when there is no immediate problem, and is it causing problems in your daily life or work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it feels completely excessive and uncontrollable, constantly looping through \"what-if\" scenarios even when there's no immediate crisis. It is definitely impacting my life, as I've started making small charting mistakes at work, turning down social invitations because I'm so drained, and my wife says I'm barely present even when we're sitting in the same room.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year, have you also had periods of feeling down, losing interest or pleasure in things, or feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I feel hopeless or deeply depressed, but I have definitely lost enjoyment in my work. I've also stopped doing things I used to love, like hiking, painting, and socializing, because I am constantly too worried, keyed up, and exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks where you felt depressed most of the day or lost interest/pleasure in nearly everything, not just avoided activities because of worry?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really felt depressed or lost interest in that way. My lack of engagement in things like hiking or painting is purely because I am constantly exhausted and too consumed by worry about the practice, rather than feeling down.\n\nDoctor: Have you started, stopped, or changed any medications, caffeine, alcohol, or other substances around the time this worry began, or do you have any thyroid or other medical problems?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't made any changes to my medications, caffeine, or alcohol intake around that time. I also don't have any thyroid issues or other medical conditions that I'm aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that the worry is happening across several different areas of life, such as finances, family, health, and daily responsibilities, rather than being focused on just one issue?"

I am just too wound up to ever relax, though I don't really recall having any specific muscle tension.\n\nDoctor: When you’re not actively worrying, do you still feel physically tense, restless, or unable to relax most days?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I'm not quite sure if I feel physically tense or restless in that way, but I definitely feel constantly "keyed up." It's just very difficult for me to ever relax or turn my mind off, no matter what I'm doing.

I've also stopped doing things I used to love, like hiking, painting, and socializing, because I am constantly too worried, keyed up, and exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks where you felt depressed most of the day or lost interest/pleasure in nearly everything, not just avoided activities because of worry?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really felt depressed or lost interest in that way. My lack of engagement in things like hiking or painting is purely because I am constantly exhausted and too consumed by worry about the practice, rather than feeling down.\n\nDoctor: Have you started, stopped, or changed any medications, caffeine, alcohol, or other substances around the time this worry began, or do you have any thyroid or other medical problems?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't made any changes to my medications, caffeine, or alcohol intake around that time. I also don't have any thyroid issues or other medical conditions that I'm aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that the worry is happening across several different areas of life, such as finances, family, health, and daily responsibilities, rather than being focused on just one issue?\n\nPatient: Yes, while it starts with the practice, the worry definitely spills over into several other areas. I find myself constantly worrying about our financial future and whether we'll have enough for retirement, as well as my responsibility to keep my staff and hygienists secure. It has gotten to the point where I'm second-guessing almost every daily decision I make.\n\nDoctor: Do you also get physical symptoms when you worry, such as muscle tension, feeling restless or on edge, fatigue, or trouble relaxing?\n\nPatient: I feel constantly keyed up and on edge, and my batteries are so drained that even simple things like walking the dog or setting up my painting gear feel like a massive effort. I am just too wound up to ever relax, though I don't really recall having any specific muscle tension.\n\nDoctor: When you’re not actively worrying, do you still feel physically tense, restless, or unable to relax most days?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure if I feel physically tense or restless in that way, but I definitely feel constantly \"keyed up.\" It's just very difficult for me to ever relax or turn my mind off, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent or ongoing medical problems, medication changes, or increased use of caffeine, alcohol, or other substances that could be contributing to the anxiety and poor sleep?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any major ongoing medical issues, and my health has been pretty stable overall. I also haven't changed any medications or increased my use of caffeine, alcohol, or other substances."
  }

It really started when I began planning my retirement and trying to arrange the practice buy-in with the younger dentists, and it hasn't let up since.\n\nDoctor: When you’re worrying, do you also get symptoms like restlessness, feeling on edge, muscle tension, trouble concentrating, irritability, or sleep problems?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. I'm barely sleeping because I lie awake running numbers and wake up at 3:00 AM unable to drift back off, leaving me feeling constantly keyed up and physically drained. My concentration has also taken a hit; I'll actually lose my train of thought or go completely blank right in the middle of a dental procedure.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have sudden episodes of intense fear or panic that peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, shortness of breath, chest pain, or feeling like something terrible is about to happen?\n\nPatient: No, I don't experience those sudden, intense spikes of panic or physical symptoms like chest pain and shortness of breath. My anxiety is more of a constant, persistent background worry and a mind that just won't stop racing. It is a steady, ongoing pressure rather than sudden bursts of terror.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the worry is excessive and hard to control even when there is no immediate problem, and is it causing problems in your daily life or work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it feels completely excessive and uncontrollable, constantly looping through \"what-if\" scenarios even when there's no immediate crisis. It is definitely impacting my life, as I've started making small charting mistakes at work, turning down social invitations because I'm so drained, and my wife says I'm barely present even when we're sitting in the same room.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year, have you also had periods of feeling down, losing interest or pleasure in things, or feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I feel hopeless or deeply depressed, but I have definitely lost enjoyment in my work. I've also stopped doing things I used to love, like hiking, painting, and socializing, because I am constantly too worried, keyed up, and exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks where you felt depressed most of the day or lost interest/pleasure in nearly everything, not just avoided activities because of worry?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really felt depressed or lost interest in that way. My lack of engagement in things like hiking or painting is purely because I am constantly exhausted and too consumed by worry about the practice, rather than feeling down.\n\nDoctor: Have you started, stopped, or changed any medications, caffeine, alcohol, or other substances around the time this worry began, or do you have any thyroid or other medical problems?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't made any changes to my medications, caffeine, or alcohol intake around that time. I also don't have any thyroid issues or other medical conditions that I'm aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that the worry is happening across several different areas of life, such as finances, family, health, and daily responsibilities, rather than being focused on just one issue?\n\nPatient: Yes, while it starts with the practice, the worry definitely spills over into several other areas. I find myself constantly worrying about our financial future and whether we'll have enough for retirement, as well as my responsibility to keep my staff and hygienists secure. It has gotten to the point where I'm second-guessing almost every daily decision I make.\n\nDoctor: Do you also get physical symptoms when you worry, such as muscle tension, feeling restless or on edge, fatigue, or trouble relaxing?\n\nPatient: I feel constantly keyed up and on edge, and my batteries are so drained that even simple things like walking the dog or setting up my painting gear feel like a massive effort. I am just too wound up to ever relax, though I don't really recall having any specific muscle tension.\n\nDoctor: When you’re not actively worrying, do you still feel physically tense, restless, or unable to relax most days?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure if I feel physically tense or restless in that way, but I definitely feel constantly \"keyed up.\" It's just very difficult for me to ever relax or turn my mind off, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent or ongoing medical problems, medication changes, or increased use of caffeine, alcohol, or other substances that could be contributing to the anxiety and poor sleep?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any major ongoing medical issues, and my health has been pretty stable overall. I also haven't changed any medications or increased my use of caffeine, alcohol, or other substances.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have sudden, unexpected panic attacks where intense fear peaks within minutes and comes with symptoms like a racing heart, shortness of breath, chest pain, trembling, or feeling like you might die or lose control?\n\nPatient: No, I don't experience those sudden, intense panic attacks at all. My anxiety is much more of a constant, steady level of worry and tension that I just can't seem to shut off. It is an ongoing, daily struggle rather than sudden bursts of fear.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a traumatic event, and if so, do you now have nightmares, flashbacks, or try to avoid reminders of it?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced any traumatic events in my life. I don't have any nightmares or flashbacks, and there is nothing like that I am trying to avoid."
  }
]

My concentration has also taken a hit; I'll actually lose my train of thought or go completely blank right in the middle of a dental procedure.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have sudden episodes of intense fear or panic that peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, shortness of breath, chest pain, or feeling like something terrible is about to happen?\n\nPatient: No, I don't experience those sudden, intense spikes of panic or physical symptoms like chest pain and shortness of breath. My anxiety is more of a constant, persistent background worry and a mind that just won't stop racing. It is a steady, ongoing pressure rather than sudden bursts of terror.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the worry is excessive and hard to control even when there is no immediate problem, and is it causing problems in your daily life or work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it feels completely excessive and uncontrollable, constantly looping through \"what-if\" scenarios even when there's no immediate crisis. It is definitely impacting my life, as I've started making small charting mistakes at work, turning down social invitations because I'm so drained, and my wife says I'm barely present even when we're sitting in the same room.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year, have you also had periods of feeling down, losing interest or pleasure in things, or feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I feel hopeless or deeply depressed, but I have definitely lost enjoyment in my work. I've also stopped doing things I used to love, like hiking, painting, and socializing, because I am constantly too worried, keyed up, and exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks where you felt depressed most of the day or lost interest/pleasure in nearly everything, not just avoided activities because of worry?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really felt depressed or lost interest in that way. My lack of engagement in things like hiking or painting is purely because I am constantly exhausted and too consumed by worry about the practice, rather than feeling down.\n\nDoctor: Have you started, stopped, or changed any medications, caffeine, alcohol, or other substances around the time this worry began, or do you have any thyroid or other medical problems?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't made any changes to my medications, caffeine, or alcohol intake around that time. I also don't have any thyroid issues or other medical conditions that I'm aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that the worry is happening across several different areas of life, such as finances, family, health, and daily responsibilities, rather than being focused on just one issue?\n\nPatient: Yes, while it starts with the practice, the worry definitely spills over into several other areas. I find myself constantly worrying about our financial future and whether we'll have enough for retirement, as well as my responsibility to keep my staff and hygienists secure. It has gotten to the point where I'm second-guessing almost every daily decision I make.\n\nDoctor: Do you also get physical symptoms when you worry, such as muscle tension, feeling restless or on edge, fatigue, or trouble relaxing?\n\nPatient: I feel constantly keyed up and on edge, and my batteries are so drained that even simple things like walking the dog or setting up my painting gear feel like a massive effort. I am just too wound up to ever relax, though I don't really recall having any specific muscle tension.\n\nDoctor: When you’re not actively worrying, do you still feel physically tense, restless, or unable to relax most days?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure if I feel physically tense or restless in that way, but I definitely feel constantly \"keyed up.\" It's just very difficult for me to ever relax or turn my mind off, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent or ongoing medical problems, medication changes, or increased use of caffeine, alcohol, or other substances that could be contributing to the anxiety and poor sleep?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any major ongoing medical issues, and my health has been pretty stable overall. I also haven't changed any medications or increased my use of caffeine, alcohol, or other substances.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have sudden, unexpected panic attacks where intense fear peaks within minutes and comes with symptoms like a racing heart, shortness of breath, chest pain, trembling, or feeling like you might die or lose control?\n\nPatient: No, I don't experience those sudden, intense panic attacks at all. My anxiety is much more of a constant, steady level of worry and tension that I just can't seem to shut off. It is an ongoing, daily struggle rather than sudden bursts of fear.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a traumatic event, and if so, do you now have nightmares, flashbacks, or try to avoid reminders of it?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced any traumatic events in my life. I don't have any nightmares or flashbacks, and there is nothing like that I am trying to avoid.\n\nDoctor: When you’re worrying, do you also have symptoms like muscle tension, feeling restless or on edge, or trouble relaxing most days?\n\nPatient: Yes, I definitely feel restless and constantly on edge, and I find it extremely difficult to relax or shut my mind off, even when I'm at home. However, I haven't really noticed any significant muscle tension."
  }

Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).
